 TA-54 Flanged Tri um Waste Container Opera ons

Execu ve Summary

In September 2025, pressure mi ga on opera ons began on the four Flanged Tri um Waste
Containers (FTWCs) that had been in storage at Los Alamos Na onal Laboratory (LANL),
Technical Area (TA-) 54, Area G, Building 1028. Each FTWC was opened at TA-54 and put into a
stable configura on, then containers were moved to LANL’s Weapons Engineering Tri um
Facility (WETF) for further processing in prepara on for shipment oﬀ-site. Over the course of 13
opera onal days, the project had air emissions of 123 curies of tri um (hydrogen-3). The
radiological dose to the hypothe cal maximally exposed individual (MEI) member of the public
from these emissions was 0.012 millirem. This is well within the limits established by the
Environmental Protec on Agency (EPA), which allows 10 millirem per year to any member of
the public. Naturally occurring background radia on in Northern New Mexico averages about
300-400 millirem per year to residents.

This report is intended to briefly summarize opera ons that took place in September and
October 2025, provide documenta on of the radionuclide air emissions from these opera ons,
and summarize the oﬀ-site dose consequence to the public that resulted from these air
emissions. Informal no fica ons addressing these areas were made via email to the EPA and
NMED throughout the project, and summaries of these data were also posted on the LANL
external web site1 for FTWC opera ons.

Introduc on

The FTWC pressure mi ga on project has been in the planning and prepara on phase since
before 2018. In addi on to the normal opera onal readiness ac vi es coordinated by the
Department of Energy (DOE), this opera on was coordinated with Region 6 of the EPA and with
the New Mexico Environment Department (NMED). The EPA regulates2 air emissions of
radionuclides from DOE facili es, and LANL’s compliance with these rules is managed by the
Radioac ve Air Emissions Management (RAEM) team within the Environmental Protec on &
Compliance Division, Compliance Programs Group (EPC-CP). EPA approval was granted in two

Air emissions from the FTWC pressure mi ga on opera ons were con nuously monitored in

This report (Volume 1) will be the first of two reports and will address exhaust stack emissions

from the addi onal “field bubblers.” The second report (Volume 2) will focus on the results of
soil sampling that was performed in areas around TA-54 and on the rou ne tri um air sampling
using silica gel that is part of LANL’s ambient air monitoring network surrounding Area G.
Analyses of these media have a longer laboratory turnaround me than the bubbler samples.
The second report will also explore modeling data in more detail, comparing actual downwind
air concentra on measurements with predicted levels using the EPA’s prescribed plume model,
CAP88.

day. These emissions data were based on the real- me ion chamber readings, and doses were

Prior to opera ons, the plan was to use the stack bubbler as the sampler of record. The
bubblers are well understood, used at other LANL facili es, and generate a sample that can be
repeatedly analyzed if needed. Throughout the project, the RAEM team compared the ion
chamber data and the stack bubbler data for each day. Early on, the stack bubbler data and ion
chamber agreed quite well. Later in the project, we noted that emissions calculated by the ion
chamber were higher than those calculated using the stack bubbler. We are working with the
instrument manufacturer to explore possible reasons for the varia on. To ensure that
emissions and doses were not under-reported, we decided to use the more conserva ve
(higher) of the two instruments’ calculated emissions values whenever the stack bubbler and
ion chamber showed significant diﬀerences (e.g., more than 1 curie diﬀerence).

This decision to use the conserva ve emissions data was made in early October, in the me
interval a er ven ng and evacua on of the FTWCs were completed but prior to their shipment
to WETF for final waste processing. As such, there were mes when the daily summaries of air
emissions and dose numbers that were emailed to EPA representa ves are diﬀerent than the
numbers documented in this report. The values presented in this document in Tables 1 and 2
represent the numbers that will be reported to EPA as part of the 2025 annual emissions and
dose repor ng process.

FTWC Processing General Steps

A generalized summary of the FTWC processing steps appears below; this is used as a reference
in the daily opera ons notes later in the report.

 1. Prepara on: open overpack 85-gallon drum lid; mount the ven ng rig onto the
 FTWC; leak check the vent rig to ensure it is sealed to FTWC.
 2. Open FTWC headspace into vent rig – this allows us to measure any pressure
 buildup.
 3. Open the vent rig to the stack; this will vent the FTWC down to ambient air pressure.
 This step can result in air emissions from built-up pressure within the FTWC.
 4. Remove vent rig and mount the pressure monitoring manifold (PMM) onto the
 FTWC. During the changeover, the FTWC headspace is briefly open to the ambient
 air. Any emissions will s ll be pulled through the monitored exhaust system.
 5. Pressurize FTWC with helium to 15 psi (verify leak- ght); this is a stable condi on for
 observa on and extended storage within TA-54 Building 1028.
 6. Bleed oﬀ helium pressure within FTWC/PMM. This step can result in air emissions as
 the helium pressure is removed. Tri um from the contaminated parts within the
 FTWC can go airborne and bleed out with the helium.
 7. Evacuate FTWC down to ~1/3 atmosphere for long-term observa on, storage, and
 movement. This step can result in air emissions; the vacuum pump is pulling on a
 contaminated system.
 8. Move FTWCs (one at a me) from Area G to the LANL tri um facility. If pressure
 buildup is observed before moving (at Area G) or a er moving (at WETF), we will
 perform evacua on process #7 again on aﬀected FTWC(s).

FTWC Ven la on and Emissions Monitoring

The FTWC ven la on system consists of a flexible exhaust duct within Building 1028, connected
to a long rigid duct outside 1028. The rigid duct connects to a blower with a short exhaust
stack. The flex duct inlet within 1028 allows reposi oning of the duct inlet over the FTWC being
processed that day. Also, the flex duct inlet can be lowered directly over the FTWC when the
vent rig is removed and before the PMM is installed; this ensures that any tri um escaping the
FTWC is pulled into the vent system and emissions are monitored. The flex duct provides
general room ven la on within Building 1028, with suﬃcient flow to pull about one air change
per minute within the building. Figure 2 shows a line schema c of the ven la on system.

The vent rig and PMM are connected to a vent cart containing the AL-M1 molecular sieve
system which can capture tri um vapor (HTO). The AL-M1 is used in steps 3 and 6, above. The
vent cart also has the vacuum pump used in step 7 above. Step 7 will bypass the AL-M1; pulling
a vacuum on a poten ally contaminated molecular sieve system may cause any tri um
adsorbed onto the molecular sieve to be released back into the air stream. The discharge from
the vent cart (a er either the AL-M1 or the vacuum pump) is then routed to the rigid exhaust
duct.

The two monitoring systems (ion chamber and stack bubbler) pull air samples from the duct
before the air enters the blower; the monitoring instruments will, therefore, measure airborne
tri um coming either through the vent cart or entering the duct through the open flex duct inlet
inside Building 1028.

At the end of each workday, the exhaust stack fan is turned oﬀ, ven la on ports are capped,
and Building 1028 is closed. There is no poten al for emissions overnight in these situa ons.

Emissions Monitoring Methods & Instrument Sensi vi es

The ion chamber used for the FTWC opera ons was a Mirion Ionix BM3 tri um monitor,
displaying tri um concentra ons within the exhaust duct in units of microcuries per cubic
meter. EPC-CP staﬀ monitored the instrument and took measurements throughout the project.
When the reading was stable, we took less frequent measurements; when the emissions levels
were rapidly changing, we took measurements as o en as every 30 seconds. We tried to use
the bounding (maximum) concentra on reading for each interval. Air concentra ons were
mul plied by the stack flow rate and the applicable me interval (e.g., 5 minutes) to determine
the accumulated emissions during that interval. The rolling total emissions (curies) were logged
to track emissions for the day. The emissions were recorded using a spreadsheet template with
a hard copy paper backup. The Ionix instrument does maintain an electronic log of the readings,
but it is not accessible in real me. We compared one day’s log with the spreadsheet record
and instrument’s electronic log, and the emissions were consistent. The background reading of
the bubbler was about 9 microcuries per liter, which corresponded to about 0.2 curies over the

 Page 6 of 21

course of a typical day’s opera ons. The daily emissions spreadsheet logs using the ion
chamber are shown in Appendix A.

The typical bubbler tri um sampler collects tri um from a sample of the exhaust air stream in a
series of six vials filled with ethylene glycol. The vials are designated A through F. Tri um in
water vapor form (HTO) in the stack sample air stream will absorb into the first three vials (A-B-
C). This completely dries out the air stream, and the air stream then passes through a high-
temperature catalyst. Within the catalyst, tri um in elemental gas phase (HT) or other possible
chemical forms is converted into water phase. The air stream then passes through the final
three vials (D-E-F) where the newly formed water vapor is absorbed into the glycol in these
vials. A er the sample vials are removed from the bubbler at the end of the day, an aliquot of
each vial is analyzed using liquid scin lla on technology to determine the quan ty of tri um in
the vials. Vials A-B-C represent the collected tri um vapor (HTO), while vials D-E-F represent
tri um in elemental gas phase (HT). The tri um collected in the vials is then mul plied by the
ra o of stack flow to the sample flow; this final value represents the total emissions of HTO and
HT from the stack for that sample period.

Bubblers are the standard for measuring emissions of airborne tri um from nuclear facili es
since the instrument provides a reproducible sample and will discriminate between HTO and HT
forms of tri um. The collec on eﬃciency of each individual vial is typically over 90%; the three
vials combined give collec on eﬃciency of over 99.9%. Also, liquid scin lla on coun ng is a
very high-resolu on analysis; the minimum detectable ac vity is typically be er than 0.01
microcuries per liter of analyzed sample, corresponding to an emissions level of less than 0.001
curie per day. When used to measure ambient air (such as the downwind “field bubblers,”
described later), the bubbler can measure about 500 picocuries per cubic meter over a week;
while this is not as low as a typical Airnet sta on (which measures 20 microcuries or less over
the course of a year), the bubbler does measure both HTO and HT, while Airnet sta ons just
measure HTO vapor.

The bubbler used for FTWC opera ons on the first several days was a Ludlum 336 tri um
collector. A er day 6, the sample flow rate display started behaving erra cally, so we switched
to an older Ortec 1700 bubbler for the remaining opera ons. Both units operate in an iden cal
manner as described above.

The only diﬀerence in opera on between FTWC stack bubbler sampling and our typical tri um
facility bubbler opera on was the use of dis lled water in Vial C; consulta ons with subject
ma er experts (SMEs) at LANL suggested that using dis lled water in Vial C would rehydrate the
sample air stream and op mize the transmi al of HT gas (a er conversion to HTO in the
catalyst). The SMEs’ concern was that in an extremely dry air stream, the HT-to-HTO converted
vapor can plate out on the catalyst and take me to migrate oﬀ the catalyst for collec on in vials

 Page 7 of 21

D-E-F. In normal bubbler opera ons where samples are run for a week and subsequent samples
are immediately installed, this is not an issue. However, for shorter term FTWC opera ons (less
than a day at a me) that can have mul ple days between sampling events, it was hypothesized
that such possible plate-out in the catalyst might result in underes ma ng emissions of HT gas.
As such, using dis lled water in Vial C will rehydrate the sample air stream before the catalyst
and op mize rapid movement of the newly converted HT-to-HTO vapor for immediate collec on
in vials D-E-F. This issue was not a factor for the downwind field bubblers and their longer
sample run mes; they used ethylene glycol in all six vials. Using glycol in the field bubblers was
also necessary due to their extended run me; dis lled water would evaporate a er more than
a few days in the arid environment.

The stack bubbler sample calcula ons are included with each day’s opera onal forms in
Appendix A; Appendix B shows the calcula ons for the downwind field bubblers.

The stack flow was determined by using a calibrated duct flow meter as shown near the top of
Figure 2. This flow meter is a Sierra 620s, sized for the 10 inch duct used in the FTWC
opera ons. The meter reads out in velocity at sea level; the process to convert this informa on
to a volumetric flow rate at al tude is described in detail in a 2019 protocol document.8 For
calcula ng real- me emissions on the ion chamber, a bounding flow rate (highest) was used for
the me period of interest and flow configura on. If the flow changed throughout the day’s
opera on, the flow rate used in the ion chamber calcula ons was changed accordingly, using a
conserva ve es mate of flow at all mes. For the bubbler emissions calcula ons, only a single
value is used in the calcula on at the end of the day. We therefore used the single highest flow
represen ng opera ons for that day; this provides a conserva ve es mate of emissions for that
instrument’s calcula on. Finally, for CAP88 plume modeling, a lower stack flow rate results in
higher oﬀ-site doses; therefore, each day’s CAP88 plume model used the lowest flow rate
measured that day. Collec vely, these assump ons ensured that we did not underes mate the
calcula on of air emissions or public dose. The flow calcula ons were done in a spreadsheet
and hand-checked; these spreadsheet calcula ons of flow rate appears in each day’s
documenta on packages in Appendix A.

Day 03 – Wednesday, September 17, 2025

 This day was focused on bleeding oﬀ the helium pressure within FTWC 226 (Step 6). We
 measured low emissions during this opera on. Later in the day, we proceeded to Step 7,

 At one point in the opera on, radiological control technicians measured concentra ons
 of tri um in the ambient air immediately around Building 1028 and iden fied a loose
 fi ng on the line between the pump and the exhaust duct. This leak represents a
 poten al unmonitored release from the process. The fi ng was ghtened and the leak
 was resolved.

 To quan fy this poten al release, we first determined the total amount of me the
 vacuum pump was opera ng before the issue was discovered and fixed; this was a 95-
 minute me interval (17:00 to 18:35). We then assumed the concentra on for this
 en re me interval was equal to the worst-case concentra on measured earlier in the
 day during the helium pressure ven ng. Finally, we applied the conserva ve exhaust
 flow rate with this maximum air concentra on and me interval to calculate a maximum
 of 12.2 curies of tri um that could have been released during this interval.

 This evalua on was discussed with the opera ons lead for the FTWC project, the on-site
 representa ve from the EPA, and the on-site representa ve from the Defense Nuclear
 Facility Safety Board. All par es concurred that this method provides a bounding
 es mate of any poten al release. We did measure stack emissions during this me
 interval as well – this indicates that at least some por on of the vacuum pump exhaust
 was going through the monitored stack and, therefore, is double counted by adding the
 12.2 curies that were calculated to the monitored emissions for that me frame. We
 administra vely declared the 12.2 unmonitored curies to be HTO vapor, since it has a
 higher dose conversion factor in CAP88 than HT gas. Later, it was noted that the bubbler
 showed pure HT gas, meaning it’s again conserva ve to assume that this 12.2 curies is

 HTO vapor; in reality, the emission was likely pure HT gas. The calcula on appears with

 At the end of Tuesday’s opera ons, the display on the bubbler flow indicator failed. The

 ion chamber measured 1.50 curies, and the bubbler measured 0.530 curies. Using the
 more conserva ve ion chamber data, the MEI dose from today’s opera on is 0.000083
 millirem, to a receptor southwest of Area G. For the fourth consecu ve day, winds were
 consistently blowing back across the Laboratory, minimizing the oﬀ-site doses from
 these opera ons. The rolling total MEI dose remains 0.0035 millirem to a residence east
 of Area G.

Day 09 –Monday, September 29, 2025

 On Day 09, we processed the final container, FTWC 227. The helium pressure bleed-
 down (Step 6) took most of the morning; we measured stack tri um concentra ons of
 over 4 millicuries per cubic meter during this period. The a ernoon evacua on of the
 drum showed the highest emissions rate measured during the project, up to 129
 millicuries per cubic meter in the stack. The ion chamber measured a total of 69.52
 curies, mostly during the drum evacua on. The bubbler data showed much lower, only
 28.7 curies total. The diﬀerences between the two instruments are more pronounced at
 the higher emissions rates seen today. We are inves ga ng all possible factors for the
 discrepancy and working with the instrument vendor on this ma er. This issue will be
 more fully explored in a later report.

 Using the higher ion chamber measurements, the MEI dose from today’s opera ons was
 0.012 millirem to a receptor southeast of Area G.

Days 10-13 – Sunday, Oct. 5; Friday, Oct. 10; Saturday, Oct. 11; Sunday, Oct. 12, 2025

 The final four days of opera ons were dedicated to moving containers, one at a me and
 one per day, from Area G out to WETF. Each morning, the pressures were checked on
 the FTWCs at Area G. No container showed a rise in pressure on any day, indica ng that
 that day’s container was safe to move. Using remote handling techniques, the FTWC
 being moved that day was first moved to the edge of Building 1028, then removed from
 1028 and placed on a drum pallet, and then the drum pallet was loaded into an enclosed
 cargo container. Pressures were checked at each step. With no pressure rise indicated,
 the cargo container was closed up and the FTWC was moved to WETF. At WETF, the
 process was reversed, with the FTWC on its pallet it was unloaded from the cargo
 container and brought to ground level, and then the FTWC was removed from the pallet
 and loaded onto the WETF loading dock. These movements between the cargo
 container and the WETF dock were made with remote handling equipment. Once on
 the WETF dock, the final pressure reading was taken and, when no pressure increase
 was noted, the FTWC was moved into WETF.

                                          Page 13 of 21

 On October 5, we moved FTWC 226. FTWC 225 was moved on October 10, followed by
 FTWC 229 on October 11. The final move of FTWC 227 was performed on Sunday,
 October 12. Since FTWC pressures remained within acceptable levels for all these days,
 the FTWCs all remained sealed and no emissions occurred on these days. No
 radiological doses resulted from these last four days of opera ons. The total dose to
 the public from the FTWC opera ons was 0.0123 millirem to the MEI in the sector
 southeast of Area G.

across the en re project. Bubbler emissions in Table 1 are based on the “quick count” from
HPAL; we cross-referenced the quick reports with the full analyses and there was no significant
diﬀerences between the two reports.

 Table 1: Stack Emissions Log Table

CAP88 Dose Calcula ons

The EPA’s plume dispersion & dose assessment model, CAP88-PC, was used to calculate doses
from each day’s opera on. We ran calcula ons using actual emissions, a conserva ve stack
flow, and actual measured meteorological parameters for wind, precipita on, humidity, and
temperature. We set up each CAP88 run to evaluate doses at the nearest public receptor in

                                         Page 14 of 21

each of 16 direc on sectors (e.g., northeast, east southeast, etc.) and to the three regional
Airnet sta ons located in downwind community loca ons. The doses at all these loca ons were
loaded into a spreadsheet matrix which iden fied the maximum dose loca on for each day’s
opera on. The spreadsheet also tracked the running total maximum dose at any of the
loca ons for the dura on of the project. The final dose tracking matrix is shown in Table 2.

As men oned above, when the ion chamber and bubbler showed a significant diﬀerence, we
used the higher of the two instrument measurements. When the ion chamber data was used,
we applied the rela ve composi on of HTO and HT that was measured in the bubbler to the
gross tri um measurements made by the ion chamber.

Note in Table 2, several receptor loca ons are shown with a zero dose, shown as 0.0E+00. This
indicates one of two things; either the CAP88 calculated dose was quite low (e.g., less than
1E-10 millirem or 0.0000000001 mrem), or there was no wind recorded in that direc on for the
dura on of that day’s opera on. With no wind frequency in a specific sector, CAP88 will not
calculate a dose in that direc on.

The CAP88 Dose Calcula on Matrix appears as Table 2 on the next page. An explana on of the
diﬀerent components of the matrix appears on the page a er Table 2.

The summary of CAP88 input parameters used each day appears in Appendix C. The individual
wind files, CAP88 input files, and dose summary tables that are used to populate Table 2 are not
included with this document due to the size of these files. However, they are available upon
request.

  Sta on 392, about 2030 meters ESE of Building 1028.

Conclusions & Next Steps

The FTWC pressure mi ga on project was completed over 13 days in September & October
2025. Air emissions were fairly low; much lower than the bounding levels that were evaluated.
All emissions and doses were well within limits established by the Environmental Protec on
Agency. While the FTWCs did not have the high pressure levels that were of concern during
project planning, the opera ons team executed the FTWC handling procedure as planned to
ensure there was no risk of explosion or other hazards to workers, the public, or the
environment.

Nuclear safety opera ons are o en faced with this issue of what can appear to be overly-
conserva ve opera onal assump ons. The philosophy is that if we cannot guarantee a hazard

9
 40 CFR 61, Appendix E, Table 2. “Concentra on Levels for Environmental Compliance.”

                                                  Page 19 of 21

will not be present, steps must be taken to mi gate that hazard. Especially in this case where
no one had ever opened up this type of sealed tri um waste storage container a er 18 years of
storage, the prudent move was to assume that worst-case scenarios are present and conduct
opera ons accordingly.

This report documented the emissions from the project and dose calcula ons that were
performed each day. Further work will compare the modeled air emissions with downwind soil
sampling to evaluate the air dispersion modeling used for this project. We will also compare the
day-by-day emissions modeling with modeling runs that combine mul ple days together, in a
manner more similar to typical CAP88 analyses. Finally, we will compare field bubbler
measurements with standard Airnet sta on measurements to evaluate in-flight behavior of
elemental tri um gas emissions. Collec vely, Volumes 1 and 2 of this report will present the
“summaries of ac vi es, lessons learned, and photographic documenta on of the process” as
requested in the NMED’s Temporary Authoriza on.10

10
 ”Approval with Modifica ons of Temporary Authoriza on Request, Class 2 Permit Modifica on for Waste

 A. Spreadsheet forms for each day’s opera on. This Appendix is subdivided into ten
 sec ons, with each sec on addressing a diﬀerent day. Each sec on has several
 components, shown below. Days 10-13 are collected into a single sec on, since they
 only contain the first item below.
 1. Emissions limit calcula on, used to iden fy pause points and overall emissions
 limits for that day.
 2. Flow rate calcula on for the duct; only the first flow rate measurement for the
 day is included in this form. The flow rate is calculated to ensure suﬃcient
 dilu on of the FTWC headspace.
 3. Flow rate trending throughout the day. The flow rate calcula on from above is
 repeated as temperature and pressure change throughout the day, or as the
 exhaust duct is reconfigured throughout the day.
 4. Emissions tracking sheets. These track the ion chamber readings and provide the
 real- me indica on of air emissions from the stack. The package for September
 17 also includes the calcula on of the poten al unmonitored release.
 5. Bubbler emissions calcula on forms. These are completed at the end of each
 day, typically using the “quick count” by the LANL analy cal laboratory.
 B. Field bubbler concentra on & dose calcula on forms.
 1. Forms are summarized by week, and a separate worksheet is generated for each
 downwind field bubbler for each weekly sample. Doses are calculated, rela ve
 to the EPA’s air concentra on dose conversion factor for tri um (per 40CFR61,
 Appendix E, Table 2; 1.5E-9 curies/m3 = 10 millirem per year; or 150 pCi/m3 over
 an en re year = 1 mrem).
 C. CAP88 input parameter table.
 1. Wind file dura on for each day;
 2. Temperature, humidity, and precipita on for each day;
 3. Stack flow rate and curie emissions for each day (HTO vapor & HT gas);
 4. Loca on of that day’s MEI loca on is noted.

 Possible Unmonitored Release Page 1 of 1

Possible Unmonitored Release
9/17/2025

When FTWC ventilation system switched from helium pressure bleed-down over to active evacuation of
 FTWC 225, RCTs measured elevated tritium air concentrations around the FTWC work site.

It turns out the vacuum pump discharge line was not fully connected to the pump; a portion of the pump exhuast
 was not being returned to the stack exhaust simply being discharged to the ambient air. This
 represents a potential unmonitored release.

Bounding analysis: Start of vacuum pump operations 17:00
 Issue discovered & fixed 18:35
 total duration = 95 minutes

 Worst-case concentration noted prior to
 equipment malfunction earlier this day = 2890 microCi/m3

 Stack flow rate at the time of release = 44.4 m3/min

 Bounding quantity of tritium release = 12.2 curies

 release = concentration * flow * duration / 1E6 microCi per Ci

 Administratively considering this release to be HTO vapor; conservative
 estimate for dose calculation (HTO dose factor is slightly
 higher than HT gas).

 Using the worst-case concentration from earlier in the day and flow rate is a conservative estimate;
 the worst-case value is usually a very short-lived peak, and applying that value to the entire
 duration of the event will over-report emissions for this time period.

 While the concentration coming out of the vacuum pump may not match the concentration
 measured within the duct earlier in the day, applying the very high flow rate in the duct
 to the assumed concentration will balance things out and result in a conservative estimate
 of potential releases for this time period.

 We discussed this issue and the emissions estimate methodology with operations staff,
 with on-site EPA representative, and with the on-site DNFSB representative. All parties
 concurred that this method would be a bounding estimate of releases.

 Local RCT instrument measurements agreed with this estimate; the maximum area readings
 were about 100-200 picocuries per cubic meter, well within the assumed concentration.

 All site personnel had tritium bioassay measurements done; there were no measureable uptakes.
 Operations were paused to address the equipment issue before any subsequent drum evacuation
 operations took place.

 Dave Fuehne, RAEM team leader, 10/30/2025
